[00:37:28] These experiences have reinforced my belief in the importance of connection, compassion, and ensuring that individuals know that they are not alone when facing mental health struggles. [00:38:10] For those who may not be familiar with AFSP, we are the nation's leading suicide prevention organization dedicated to saving lives and bringing hope to those impacted by suicide. We do this through research, education, advocacy, and support.

[00:38:31] We believe that suicide can be prevented, and we each have a role to play in creating communities where people feel connected, supported, and empowered to seek help. [00:38:58] This work is made possible by an incredible network of volunteers, donors, sponsors, and community supports. This year is especially meaningful because we're celebrating the 20th anniversary of our Fairfax Out of the Darkness Walk.

Whereas sickle cell disease is an inherited blood disorder resulting from the transmission of the sickle cell gene by both

[00:43:09] parents, disproportionately affecting African American, Hispanic, Middle Eastern, Asian, and Mediterranean populations. The town hall showcased resources and emerging technologies benefiting sickle cell patients. We learned about breakthrough gene therapies and bone marrow transplantation that are significantly reducing the impacts of

[00:47:01] this disease. Gene therapies alter a patient's hemoglobin gene so that the red blood cells are less likely to become sickle-shaped, hence the name sickle cell. This can prevent or treat many of the problems associated with sickle cell disease. A bone marrow transplant is a procedure to replace abnormal bone marrow cells with new healthy bone-forming cells, blood-forming cells that can make healthy red blood cells that do not sickle. So very important. These procedures

[00:47:40] are going to hopefully lead to a cure for this disease. And I'll note a couple things. This disease is personal to me. I lost a dear friend who I'd worked with in Fairfax County for more than 10 years.

My next board matter is authorization to send a letter to the Advisory Council on Historic Preservation regarding proposed regulatory revisions implementing the National Historic Preservation Act Section 106 process.

[01:35:02] The Section 106 consultation process of the National Historic Preservation Act requires federal agencies to consult with state and local governments, tribal governments, and the public to identify historic resources, determine potential effects, and collaborate on preservation-oriented alternatives or mitigation strategies before moving forward with federal undertakings. Fairfax County has been a certified local government under this framework since 1995. This allows us to have a formal consulting party role in the review of federally connected projects which may impact historic resources, which, given our location,

[01:35:40] of course, is of critical importance to us. Recently, the Advisory Council on Historic Preservation, ACHP, proposed major changes to Section 106 regulations that, if adopted, would eliminate the requirement for consultation with state and local governments and the public.

[01:35:57] The proposed changes also narrow the definition of historic properties, removing historic landscapes such as battlefields, rural districts, and traditional cultural places from consideration altogether. These revisions represent a substantial change to the core commitment of the federal Government to Historic Preservation and Community Input into Federal Decision-Making Regarding Historic Properties.

[01:36:22] The ACHP proposed revisions to the regulations implementing the Section 106 process are currently under review by the Office of Information and Regulatory Affairs, OIRA. If the proposed regulations clear OIRA review, then they will be published in the Federal Register and begin a public comment period.

[01:36:42] Recently, a coalition of more than 600 public, private, and nonprofit organizations sent an open letter to Congress outlining their concerns regarding ACHP's proposed revisions and additional efforts to weaken Section 106. Therefore, I move that the Board of Supervisors send the attached letter to ACHP urging the Council to reconsider or withdraw the proposed regulatory changes and that the county executive direct county staff to proactively prepare a response to the changes should they be published, emphasizing the importance of tribal, state, and local government and public participation

[01:37:19] in advocating for all historic resources to be considered in the Section 106 federal review process. I'll second that motion. Discussion on the motion. Supervisor Stork? I'm happy to second that as well, but especially recognize the criticality of federal protection of national historic sites.

[01:37:41] And frankly, many of those go far beyond what is Mount Vernon or George Mason's home. Really, it's across the country. [01:38:15] We talked about Hispanic heritage, and much of that is the core of the Southwest as well. So it's a national issue. [02:25:53] I have an additional board matter, and this is review of the Fairfax County Police Department's use of Flock Safety's automated license plate readers. Over the course of the last several weeks, our offices have received hundreds of emails from residents regarding the Fairfax County Police Department's use of Flock Safety's automated license plate readers, Flock ALPRs.

[02:26:12] This is occurring as part of a nationwide conversation where the utilization of flock safety technology for public safety objectives intersects with real privacy concerns. Public safety and public trust go hand in hand, and as such, Fairfax County must join this broader conversation as well.

[02:26:29] To be clear, FCPD has taken significant steps to ensure that the 84 licensed stationary flock ALPRs in its program are utilized in a transparent manner, abide by all state and county codes, and are only utilized for legitimate law enforcement purposes and subject to a 100% audit for searches entered.

[02:26:50] FCPD also requires that those who have access to this technology to undergo training to ensure that they are following all applicable laws and doing so ethically. It's important to note that per FCPD policy, FCPD does not share any of its data with federal law enforcement agencies unless absolutely required by law. Sharing data with other Virginia law enforcement agencies is permissible, but only if there is a legitimate law enforcement need to do so. In an effort to remain transparent, FCPD also launched a public-facing website that shares information on when this technology is used, cumulative data of public safety outcomes, frequently asked questions,

[02:27:30] and more. These guardrails are vital to ensure appropriate application of the technology and maintain trust with the Fairfax County community. To date, there have been no identified instances of misuse of this technology by FCPD. To be clear, this technology has demonstrated a clear public safety benefit from November 2022 to November 4 of 2026, FCPD's Flock ALPR program has resulted in helping solve over 1,600 cases, including locating 57 missing persons, including

[02:28:03] children and elderly. 66 firearms recovered, 2.633 arrest charges, and 555, I'm sorry, 2,633 arrest charges, and 555 stolen vehicles recovered with an estimated value of over $9 million.

[02:28:20] That being said, while the Board of Supervisors and FCPD have taken action to try to ensure that this technology is used transparently and responsibly, the concerns raised by residents are clearly legitimate and understandable. Examples from around the nation where individuals or law enforcement agencies have deployed this technology for non-law enforcement purposes is alarming and concerning. Additional issues highlighted report the potential for unauthorized access of data gathered by these cameras that raise valid civil liberty concerns. While data captured by FCPD's FLOC ALPRs is stored on Fairfax County's Department of Information Technology

[02:29:00] servers and deleted permanently after 21 days, reporting from around the country and concerns from residents necessitate a broader conversation. These concerns also require assurances from FLOC safety that they cannot and do not have access to data collected from FCPD's Flock ALPR program.

[02:29:20] I also recognize that private entities such as homeowners associations and commercial property owners throughout the county utilize this technology on their own private property without county approval. Additional information could be helpful in informing decisions as it relates to the use of Flock ALPRs by private citizens and businesses. Therefore, I move that the Board of Supervisors direct FCPD to pause the installation of any new stationary flock ALPRs until the Board

[02:29:49] of Supervisors is able to discuss this matter further at a Safety and Security Committee meeting in October of 2026. FCPD should evaluate other options and report back in the committee meeting. I further move that the Board of Supervisors send a letter to Flock Safety's Chief Executive Officer to request responses to the questions posed that is substantially in the form of the letter attached to this board matter, which is attached to everyone's board matter.

[03:13:06] And so I'm going to read the motion in full because I think it will give you better context of the differences that we all can make in making sure that we have safe supervision for learning swimmers. After two decades of reductions, child drowning deaths in America are rising again, from 756 in 2019 to 865 in 2024, according to the Center for Disease Control, the bulk being children under five. Drowning is the number one cause of death for children ages one to four, and it happens in seconds, silently. As the American Academy of Pediatrics warned just this

[03:13:42] year, quick rescue can mean the difference between life, death, and lifelong disability. This underscores the importance of lifeguards focusing entirely on life-saving activities while on duty. The first line of defense is and always should be the parent or guardian supervising the learning swimmer. However, too often families expect lifeguards on duty to provide oversight to children who are still in the process of learning how to swim. Pediatricians, the National Drowning Prevention Alliance, and the Virginia Department of Health all recommend touch supervision where an adult is within arm's reach of any child who cannot swim whenever a learning swimmer is in or around

[03:14:21] water. Current Fairbanks County Health Code requires lifeguards to be free from distraction, which allows for immediate assistance to patrons in distress. The code also requires supervision of children when using a spa or pool, as does the Fairbanks County Park Authority rules, which requires supervision of children under 10 from the water or deck. There are several initiatives underway to strengthen required supervision and swimming skills. For example, modifications to our pool code to ensure learning swimmers receive direct and active supervision by a parent or guardian when in swimming pools.

[03:14:57] The Park Authority also has a Learn to Swim program for community members. While a good foundation, our programming needs to be scaled up to provide greater opportunities for our residents, especially those for whom financial barriers may prevent them from accessing community Learn to Swim classes to gain life-saving swimming skills and improve water safety techniques.

[03:15:18] Therefore, Mr. Chairman, I ask that the Board of Supervisors direct the Fairfax County Park Authority to review their water safety rules and access to learn-to-swim programs and the Health Department to complete their ongoing review of Chapter 69.1 to identify if any new requirements or revisions may be needed to increase public safety and report back by early 2027 through the appropriate committee of the Board or through a NIP with recommendations on any challenges necessary to protect our learning swimmers by early 2027.

[04:28:35] While we support long-term community planning, adding multifamily housing directly across from this high school will introduce a significant increase of multimodal traffic, daily trip generation, and vehicular density to an already overwhelmed intersection.

[04:28:56] Taking away the human traffic control as the board hopes to increase residential congestion at this exact crossing is a recipe for disaster. When you remove train crossing guards from Stringfellow Road, the entire system breaks down. Safety degrades instantly.

[04:29:15] Traffic delays spike. Automatic signals cannot dynamically adapt to real-time school rush conditions. A single pedestrian pressing the crosswalk button stops all four lanes of traffic, holding up commuters, and spilling the gridlock miles back along Stringfellow.

[04:29:33] Without manual traffic management, drivers grow frustrated, lead times doubles, and aggressive driving around the school surges. A professional crossing guard does far more than push a button. The applicant proposes to exceed the 10-year tree canopy requirement and the tree preservation target through the preservation of the resource protection area and landscaping throughout the development. [07:31:36] This is a project, this is a day, rather, that's the culmination of a process that really began back in 2022, nearly five years ago, when the property owner submitted this 41-acre assemblage for a site-specific plan amendment. A couple of years ago, they brought in EYA, one of Fairfax County's premier home builders, to partner with them on this overall project that seeks to transform this 41-acre site on the western end of the Merrifield Suburban Center into a thriving residential community that is more compatible with the county's vision for Merrifield. And so we went through a site-specific plan amendment. We are now at the end of the rezoning process, and we actually have a final development plan coming up at the end of the

[07:32:15] month in front of the Planning Commission for phase one. So things are starting to happen here, which is really, really great. The site itself, Ms. Cole covered this, but briefly, it really embodies EYA's core philosophy, which is life within walking distance. We're here on the western edge of Merrifield but we're within a half mile short walk from the Dunloring metro station within a short walk of the mosaic district so it's a site that's really ripe for residential development because residents want to be close to those places close to Metro and close to all of the amenities that Merrifield has to offer we've really taken advantage of maximizing the

[07:32:52] development potential of this site as miss Cole mentioned we have two potential development options what you have on the screen here is option one Option 1 is proposing up to a total of 1,698 residential units, just under 3 million square feet of development.

[07:33:08] As you can see here on this site plan, we've taken advantage of the site's proximity to the metro. Those buildings on the right side of the image or the northern portion of the site are the ones that are closest to the Dunloring metro station. The site, the townhouse blocks, were oriented around a network of public and private streets.

[07:33:25] We had a very collaborative process with FCDOT at the plan amendment stage to come up with the right treatment of those streets, the right orientation of the blocks, public and private streets that will ultimately provide vehicular and pedestrian connectivity to the portions of Merrifield to the east of this as the area redevelops over time.

[07:33:42] We have a network of publicly accessible open spaces. Finally, we have a public hearing to consider amendments to the code of the County of Fairfax, Virginia, Chapter 46, Health and Safety Menaces to Establish Regulations for Criminal Blight.

[08:38:42] And as soon as they are ready, it's going to take a moment here to transition. As soon as they are ready, we would welcome a county staff presentation.

[08:39:04] Good evening, ladies and gentlemen on the board. My name is Patrick Fultz. I'm an assistant county attorney, and I'll be presenting the amendment, Chapter 46, Criminal Blight Procedure.

[08:39:21] The proposed ordinance amendment derives from 15.2-907 of the Virginia Code. This section was originally enacted in 1994. The ordinance we are seeking to enact exactly tracks the enabling authority, and at least 10 other jurisdictions since 1994 have enacted full or partial ordinances based on 15.2-97, including Hampton, Portsmouth, Henrico, Fredericksburg, Suffolk, Virginia Beats, Charlottesville, and Newport News.

[08:39:50] Norfolk and Alexandria have adopted partial versions of the code encompassing certain definitions. The definition of criminal blight, according to the statute, is a condition existing on real property that endangers the public health or safety of residents of a locality and is caused by four factors.

[08:40:07] The regular presence on that property of persons in the possession or under the influence of controlled substances. The regular use of the property for the purpose of illegally possessing, manufacturing, or distributing these substances. The regular use of the property for the purpose of engaging in commercial sex acts. or the discharge of a firearm that would constitute a criminal act under either state or local code.

[08:40:30] Enforcement of this ordinance would be through a four-step process, identification and investigation of criminal blight by law enforcement. Complaints and investigations do not have to originate in law enforcement, but in order to execute the affidavit that's needed for this provision, law enforcement officers do have to be involved.

[08:40:47] The second step is a notice of criminal blight, supported by an affidavit executed by the law enforcement officer, sent to the property owner. The notice demands appropriate corrective actions from the property owner. If corrective action is not undertaken by the property owner after 30 days, a second notice is issued. It states the date on which the locality may commence corrective action or commence legal action in court to address the criminal blight condition. And if corrective action is still not undertaken by that date, the county may then undertake the corrective actions it has identified.

[08:41:22] Costs incurred by the county in undertaking corrective action must be paid by the property owner, and unpaid costs may be collected by the county in the same manner as a tax lien. If the owner undertakes timely corrective action, the county deems the criminal blight abated, closes its file, and promptly provides written notice to the property owner that the proceeding has abated satisfactorily.

[08:41:43] The benefits of this ordinance amendment include a streamlined process to address criminal blight conditions, a new civil process to address criminal blight, thus reducing burdens on law enforcement and the criminal justice system, and a process that sets clear timelines for property owners to undertake corrective actions.

[08:42:03] Thank you. That concludes my presentation. Thank you very much for that. Questions of staff, Supervisor Alcorn. I have a really dumb question. Does this replace our blight abatement program? It does not. It is a wholly separate program. Yeah, and tell me the difference between the two.

[08:42:21] When would one be used and not the other? At the risk of giving legal advice in open session. Yeah, that's fine. You don't have to do that. But is there any general distinction, or do they overlap potentially? Supervisor Alcorn, the blight abatement is directed at properties that fall into severe disrepair, a set of criteria that has to do with neglect or other similar conditions.

[08:42:49] And what we're talking about here is a set of conditions that are caused by, frankly, more intentional actions. Activity. where you've got drug activity going on on the property or any of the other things that Mr. Fultz mentioned. That's really helpful.

[08:43:05] Thank you. So in essence, different tools, but important to have both for different circumstances. Okay. Any other questions of staff? Supervisor Stork. No questions. All right. If there are no further questions, this is a public hearing, and I have no speakers who have signed up in advance to testify on this matter.

[08:43:27] there anyone here present who would like to speak on this specific subject this is a public hearing seeing and hearing no one let me ask the clerk if we have any callers there are no callers no callers closing comments and a recommendation from staff no closing comments staff recommends that the ordinance and then amendments be adopted last check questions from board members seeing and hearing on I will now close the public hearing and recognize vice chair Smith for a motion thank you mr. chairman I move that the board of if supervisors adopt the criminal blight amendments to the Code of the County of Fairfax,

[08:44:01] Chapter 46, as set forth in Attachment 2 to the board item dated September 15, 2026. I further move that the board make the amendments effective as it's 12.01 a.m. on October 1, 2026.

[08:44:16] You've heard the motion. It's been seconded by Supervisor Lusk. Discussion on the motion. Supervisor Stork. Thank you, Mr. Chairman, and I couldn't be happier. We are where we are. I introduced this board matter back in February, but I introduced it because of some severe challenges we were having in our community.

[08:44:38] One specific location, but there's been at least a couple of others where I think this could have been available to us. And previously, we had just a very, very elongated process to address not only blight, but in this case, criminal blight folks who were, or our police officers have had to be there dozens of times to try to address issues that were there, and still, really, the issues weren't addressed.

[08:45:04] Ultimately, we had to go through the criminal justice system to try to do that, which is not as clear a process as this could be because there are so many more individuals involved in that. So the county really has more tools in its tool shed, if you will, to address what I know has been at least a couple of different situations in the Mount Vernon district, something that really, truly made it difficult for the community.

[08:45:33] I mean, difficult is an understatement, made it incredibly hard for neighbors to enjoy their homes and even to feel safe in their homes.
